The\\r\\nkey to burst detection is to reduce the awareness time, i.e., the\\r\\nperiod between the burst occurrence and the awareness of water\\r\\nutilities. Although the mentioned equipment can localize leakage\\r\\nprecisely, it remains a great challenge when facing the cost of time,\\r\\nmoney, and human power, and consequently resulting in a heavy\\r\\nburden on water utilities (Li et al. 2015).\\r\\nCompared with the presented methods relying on hardware\\r\\nequipment, detection based on the monitoring data in district me-\\r\\ntering areas (DMAs) costs less and arouses wide concern in the\\r\\nwater industry for its convenience. The monitoring data reflect the\\r\\noperating states of the network. When leaks happen, the operating\\r\\npatterns of monitoring data will be different from the ones under\\r\\nnormal conditions. By mining the DMA monitoring data, leakage\\r\\ncan be identified in real-time (Romano et al. 2014). The data-driven\\r\\nmethod can be mainly divided into three types, including statistical\\r\\nprocess control (SPC), classification, and prediction-classification\\r\\ntwo-stage method (Wu and Liu 2017b). Jung et al. (2015) propose\\r\\nand demonstrate the application of several SPC methods on de-\\r\\ntecting leaks. Pressure and flow data are analyzed through standard\\r\\ndeviation, cumulative sum (CUSUM), and threshold settings to\\r\\ndetect the abnormal statistical status caused by leakages. These\\r\\nSPC-based methods have been considered as simple, concise, and\\r\\ndirect techniques for leak detection (Ahn and Jung 2019). Some\\r\\nresearchers treat leakage detection as a binary classification prob-\\r\\nlem. These are not final forecast test scores.\n\n[EDA and four figures](docs/exploratory_analysis.md) · [verified outputs](results/) · [executed notebook](notebooks/milestone2_analysis.ipynb) · [report claim checklist](evidence/report_claims.csv)\n\n## Literature and anticipated work\n\nThe [literature index](literature/README.md) exposes **47 source records in 35 groups of related works**, notes for every group, and [six detailed reviews](literature/detailed_review/). The original source is [Vrachimis et al., Dataset of BattLeDIM](https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.4017659), supported by the [2022 benchmark paper](https://doi.org/10.1061/%28ASCE%29WR.1943-5452.0001601). L-Town is a simulated drinking-water network inspired by Cyprus, released for leak detection and localization. Its measurements are simulated SCADA observations, not field data.\n\nEach year, 2018 and 2019, has 105,120 five-minute records: **210,240 timestamps and 119 numerical channels** overall. The channels are 33 pressures (m water head), three flows (m³/h), one T1 level (m) and 82 AMR consumptions (L/h). AMR is junction consumption, not pipe inflow. All eight annual tables have matching unique timestamps within their year and no missing values, as checked by [the analysis code](../src/analysis.py) and [table results](../results/table_checks.json). The benchmark describes ideal telemetry and two-decimal rounding; recorded pressure averages the preceding five minutes. These assumptions limit transfer to field sensors.\n\nThe nominal model contains 782 demand junctions, two reservoirs, one tank, 905 pipes, one pump and three PRVs. Nominal and generator parameters differ; this repository's path and sector checks use the released **nominal** `L-TOWN.inp`.\n\n## Target and selected measurements\n\nThe supervised regression label is `p_n1(u + 15 minutes)`, three records after forecast origin `u`. It is the recorded five-minute average ending at that future time, under the simulated operating and leakage conditions. There are no target classes. Leak labels are excluded from predictors. Every predictor must be available when the forecast is issued.\n\n| Selected signal | Unit | Student's engineering rationale |\n|---|---|---|\n| n1 pressure | m | History of the target pressure. |\n| n4 and n31 pressures | m | Two additional observed pressures in Area C. |\n| T1 level | m | Tank storage state at the sector boundary. |\n| PUMP_1 flow | m³/h | Measured tank inflow; may inform later storage changes. |\n| AMR_n1 demand | L/h | Consumption assigned directly to the target junction. |\n\nArea C is tank-fed and contains 92 junctions, three pressure sensors and 82 AMRs. The reduction is **119 channels → 87 Area C/boundary candidates → six signals**. The 87 consist of three pressures, one level, 82 demands and pump flow. PRV-3's sector has only one measured pressure, whereas C offers related peer measurements. A tank boundary supports a manageable feature choice; it does not establish statistical independence from upstream operation.\n\nShortest initially open pipe paths from T1 to measured nodes are **891.3208 m (n1), 573.7565 m (n4), 307.7926 m (n31)**. Dijkstra's algorithm sums nominal pipe lengths, excludes pump/PRV links and treats the pipe graph as undirected. This makes n1 the most distant of these three sensors by this definition; it does not establish a measured propagation delay or the most critical node in the whole sector.\n\n[Figure 1](../results/figures/figure1_supply_boundary.png) is an interpretive functional schematic, not a geographically accurate map or hydraulic simulation. PRV settings 40/50/35 m are nominal model values, not measurements of valve operation. Fixed PRV settings do not make remote pressures constant. [Topology outputs](../results/topology_audit.json) record the connections, settings and path calculations.\n\nThe [EDA](exploratory_analysis.md) explains why the task is feasible and persistence is demanding. [C1–C3](anticipated_contributions.md) will test whether these auxiliary signals add forecast value; correlation alone cannot answer that question.\n''')\nwrite('docs/student_contributions.md','''\n# Student contributions and AI assistance\n\n**Andres Arango Martinez is the research lead and intellectual contributor.** He conceived the direction connecting pressure forecasting with his hydraulic and control-engineering work; selected BattLeDIM/L-Town, Area C, n1 and the +15-minute horizon; chose n1/n4/n31, T1, PUMP_1 and AMR_n1 through engineering reasoning; and defined the analyses and visualizations. It checks full 2018 and 2019 table structure, but computes pressure forecasts/errors and correlations only for **January–September 2018**. Table checks do not establish that 2019 was never previously inspected.\n\n[Data loading and calculations](../src/analysis.py) use the six selected signals, without interpolation, filtering or scaling. The period is cropped before `n1.shift(-3)` creates the future label. The last three origins cannot obtain labels inside September and are dropped. This produces 78,621 pairs from 78,624 timestamps. These pairs use current inputs for descriptive correlations and persistence; they are **not** the later lagged training matrix, which will also require 55 minutes of history.\n\nFor error `e = n1(u + 15) − n1(u)`, MAE is the mean of `abs(e)` and RMSE is the square root of the mean of `e²`. Pearson correlations use the same aligned pairs. The future target is never an input predictor.\n\n| Quantity | Reproduced value |\n|---|---:|\n| Days / timestamps | 273 / 78,624 |\n| Valid +15-minute pairs | 78,621 |\n| n1 minimum / maximum | 25.67 / 29.36 m |\n| Persistence MAE | 0.05177624298851451 m |\n| Persistence RMSE | 0.06849175638676988 m |\n| Current n1 / future n1 correlation | 0.9923104288422752 |\n| T1 / future n1 correlation | 0.9230382756150443 |\n| PUMP_1 / future n1 correlation | −0.036721113537145696 |\n| AMR_n1 / future n1 correlation | −0.1573783306135834 |\n\n[Statistics](../results/eda_statistics.json) · [complete correlation table](../results/correlations.csv) · [numerical verification](../results/verification.json)\n\n## Four figures\n\n**Figure 1 · Functional supply boundary.** The [nominal model check](../src/analysis.py) identifies boundaries, pump endpoints and pressure sensors; [plotting code](../src/figures.py) lays out the interpretive diagram. The left tank/pump panels span the same total height as the three right pressure panels. The nominal controls open PUMP_1 below 2.4 m and close it above 3.9 m. The plot shows daily variation; it does not prove a causal lead from the pump to future n1.\n\n![Daily tank, pump and pressure profiles](../results/figures/figure2_daily_profiles.png)\n\n**Figure 3 · Correlations.** The gold boundary separates the future label from six current signals. Persistence's small descriptive error and high n1 autocorrelation make it a strong reference. A signal with high correlation may add little beyond n1, and a low contemporaneous correlation does not rule out useful lagged information.\n\n![Six-signal and future-label correlation matrix](../results/figures/figure3_correlations.png)\n\n**Figure 4 · Literature groups.** Counts are recomputed by joining source IDs and group assignments. Include time of day and day of week as stated in the report. Exact calendar encoding and model settings remain implementation choices. Exclude rows missing any required predictor or whose target lies outside the assigned period. Fit any Ridge scaling on training data only. Correlations and hydraulic reasoning motivate candidates; they do not establish added predictive value.\n\nThe main model, [Gradient Boosting](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.ensemble.GradientBoostingRegressor.html), adds small decision trees that correct earlier prediction errors. Persistence repeats current n1. [Ridge](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.linear_model.Ridge.html) fits a linear relationship while penalizing large coefficients. [Random Forest](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.ensemble.RandomForestRegressor.html) averages many trees. The comparison asks whether a manageable nonlinear model improves on simple alternatives; these established methods are appropriate to the course's regression and ensemble material.\n\n## Chronological evaluation\n\nTrain on January–September 2018, select settings with October–December 2018, then refit on eligible 2018 rows and assess 2019. Since 2019 has already been inspected in limited ways, call it the **final chronological assessment**, not an untouched holdout. Do not choose settings using 2019 outcomes.\n\nAll models must predict the same eligible rows and the same recorded target. Report **MAE** and **RMSE** in metres. MAE gives average error magnitude; RMSE penalizes large errors more strongly. A boosting advantage requires lower MAE than all three specified comparators. C3 keeps the model family, calendar inputs and evaluation rows fixed so that its full-versus-reduced comparison answers the additional-signal question. Report RMSE tradeoffs and negative findings.\n\nExpected outputs are an annotated preparation/model notebook, a comparison table and forecast/error plots. These remain anticipated outputs. Nominal and generator parameters differ; this repository's path and sector checks use the released **nominal** `L-TOWN.inp`.\n\n## Target and selected measurements\n\nThe supervised regression label is `p_n1(u + 15 minutes)`, three records after forecast origin `u`. It is the recorded five-minute average ending at that future time, under the simulated operating and leakage conditions. There are no target classes. Leak labels are excluded from predictors. Every predictor must be available when the forecast is issued.\n\n| Selected signal | Unit | Student's engineering rationale |\n|---|---|---|\n| n1 pressure | m | History of the target pressure. |\n| n4 and n31 pressures | m | Two additional observed pressures in Area C. |\n| T1 level | m | Tank storage state at the sector boundary. |\n| PUMP_1 flow | m³/h | Measured tank inflow; may inform later storage changes. |\n| AMR_n1 demand | L/h | Consumption assigned directly to the target junction. |\n\nArea C is tank-fed and contains 92 junctions, three pressure sensors and 82 AMRs. The reduction is **119 channels → 87 Area C/boundary candidates → six signals**. The 87 consist of three pressures, one level, 82 demands and pump flow. PRV-3's sector has only one measured pressure, whereas C offers related peer measurements. A tank boundary supports a manageable feature choice; it does not establish statistical independence from upstream operation.\n\nShortest initially open pipe paths from T1 to measured nodes are **891.3208 m (n1), 573.7565 m (n4), 307.7926 m (n31)**. Dijkstra's algorithm sums nominal pipe lengths, excludes pump/PRV links and treats the pipe graph as undirected. This makes n1 the most distant of these three sensors by this definition; it does not establish a measured propagation delay or the most critical node in the whole sector.\n\n[Figure 1](../results/figures/figure1_supply_boundary.png) is an interpretive functional schematic, not a geographically accurate map or hydraulic simulation. PRV settings 40/50/35 m are nominal model values, not measurements of valve operation. Fixed PRV settings do not make remote pressures constant. [Topology outputs](../results/topology_audit.json) record the connections, settings and path calculations.\n\nThe [EDA](exploratory_analysis.md) explains why the task is feasible and persistence is demanding. [C1–C3](anticipated_contributions.md) will test whether these auxiliary signals add forecast value; correlation alone cannot answer that question.\n''')\nwrite('docs/student_contributions.md','''\n# Student contributions and AI assistance\n\n**Andres Arango Martinez is the research lead and intellectual contributor.** He conceived the direction connecting pressure forecasting with his hydraulic and control-engineering work; selected BattLeDIM/L-Town, Area C, n1 and the +15-minute horizon; chose n1/n4/n31, T1, PUMP_1 and AMR_n1 through engineering reasoning; and defined the analyses and visualizations. He directed the literature search, screening, focused reading and technical interpretation, analyzed the findings, made the research decisions, and established the proposed model comparisons and additional-signal question.\n\nAI assisted writing and language editing, organization of references, retrieval and checking of sources, and generation and execution of analytical code under the student's instructions. It also assisted the preparation of this reproducibility repository. This statement does not assert that the student manually wrote every line of code or independently ran each recorded command.\n\nExternal data, publications, author code and their reported performance retain their original authorship. The numerical outputs here are executions of the student-directed descriptive analysis using the official source data. The original authors' learning models were not trained or reproduced. Gradient Boosting, Ridge, Random Forest and the C3 comparison remain proposed work.\n\nThe unchanged [submitted reports](../reports/) preserve the actual course submissions. This repository supplies traceable supporting evidence and clarification; it does not rewrite those submissions or claim that newly recorded executions occurred before submission.\n''')\nwrite('docs/exploratory_analysis.md','''\n# Completed exploratory analysis\n\nThis page supports [Milestone 2, Sections 1.1–1.3 and Figures 1–4](../reports/Milestone2.pdf). See the [contribution statement](student_contributions.md) for research direction and AI-assisted implementation. All values below are descriptive reproductions; no learned model has been fitted.\n\n## Source and calculations\n\nThe pipeline verifies the eleven files in the [official-source manifest](../data/source_manifest.json): eight annual SCADA tables, the nominal model, sensor configuration and README. It checks full 2018 and 2019 table structure, but computes pressure forecasts/errors and correlations only for **January–September 2018**. Table checks do not establish that 2019 was never previously inspected.\n\n[Data loading and calculations](../src/analysis.py) use the six selected signals, without interpolation, filtering or scaling. The period is cropped before `n1.shift(-3)` creates the future label. The last three origins cannot obtain labels inside September and are dropped. This produces 78,621 pairs from 78,624 timestamps. These pairs use current inputs for descriptive correlations and persistence; they are **not** the later lagged training matrix, which will also require 55 minutes of history.\n\nFor error `e = n1(u + 15) − n1(u)`, MAE is the mean of `abs(e)` and RMSE is the square root of the mean of `e²`. Pearson correlations use the same aligned pairs. The future target is never an input predictor.\n\n| Quantity | Reproduced value |\n|---|---:|\n| Days / timestamps | 273 / 78,624 |\n| Valid +15-minute pairs | 78,621 |\n| n1 minimum / maximum | 25.67 / 29.36 m |\n| Persistence MAE | 0.05177624298851451 m |\n| Persistence RMSE | 0.06849175638676988 m |\n| Current n1 / future n1 correlation | 0.9923104288422752 |\n| T1 / future n1 correlation | 0.9230382756150443 |\n| PUMP_1 / future n1 correlation | −0.036721113537145696 |\n| AMR_n1 / future n1 correlation | −0.1573783306135834 |\n\n[Statistics](../results/eda_statistics.json) · [complete correlation table](../results/correlations.csv) · [numerical verification](../results/verification.json)\n\n## Four figures\n\n**Figure 1 · Functional supply boundary.** The [nominal model check](../src/analysis.py) identifies boundaries, pump endpoints and pressure sensors; [plotting code](../src/figures.py) lays out the interpretive diagram. Arrows summarize the intended supply route, not solved flows or signal delays.\n\n![Functional hydraulic supply boundary](../results/figures/figure1_supply_boundary.png)\n\n**Figure 2 · Daily profiles.** Thin lines show each of the 273 days; the red line is the mean at each clock time. The left tank/pump panels span the same total height as the three right pressure panels. The nominal controls open PUMP_1 below 2.4 m and close it above 3.9 m. The plot shows daily variation; it does not prove a causal lead from the pump to future n1.\n\n![Daily tank, pump and pressure profiles](../results/figures/figure2_daily_profiles.png)\n\n**Figure 3 · Correlations.** The gold boundary separates the future label from six current signals. Persistence's small descriptive error and high n1 autocorrelation make it a strong reference. A signal with high correlation may add little beyond n1, and a low contemporaneous correlation does not rule out useful lagged information.\n\n![Six-signal and future-label correlation matrix](../results/figures/figure3_correlations.png)\n\n**Figure 4 · Literature groups.** Counts are recomputed by joining source IDs and group assignments. Gold bars identify selected groups within each category. They are subsets, not extra studies. The five main objectives are not the number of individual papers. A diagnostic method can contain a future-pressure step while remaining in the leak category.\n\n![Historical literature category counts](../results/figures/figure4_historical_screening.png)\n\nThe [screening notes](../literature/screening_notes.md) distinguish brief screening from the six detailed readings. Counting code validates consistency, not search completeness or all scholarly classifications. The [execution manifest](../results/run_manifest.json) records inputs, code, environment and outputs. [Submitted image bytes](../evidence/submitted_figures/) are retained separately: regenerated figures support the same content with portable fonts; pixel identity is not claimed.\n''')\nwrite('docs/anticipated_contributions.md','''\n# Your anticipated contributions\n\nThis is the proposal in [Milestone 2, Section 3](../reports/Milestone2.pdf#page=4). **The learning experiments have not been implemented or evaluated.** The completed persistence calculation is descriptive EDA, not a final model result.\n\nThe intended contribution is a reproducible comparison for recorded **n1 pressure at +15 minutes on the released SCADA**. The six reviewed methods include next-step pressure prediction, longer-window head forecasting, current-state reconstruction and hydraulic diagnosis. None supplies a directly comparable evaluation for this precise target, signals and chronological protocol. This is a bounded observation about the reviewed collection, not worldwide priority or a new algorithm. Boosting already appears in L-Town leakage classification; the novelty must be judged through the specific forecasting comparison and what it establishes about additional signals.\n\n| Submitted contribution | What will be prepared or compared | How its value will be judged |\n|---|---|---|\n| **C1 · Prepared forecast data** | Match available past measurements with n1 three five-minute readings ahead. | Correct time alignment, units and period boundaries; no future values or leakage labels in inputs. |\n| **C2 · Model comparison** | Gradient Boosting against persistence, Ridge and Random Forest. | Lower MAE on identical forecast rows; report RMSE and any tradeoffs. |\n| **C3 · Value of extra signals** | Full-signal Gradient Boosting against Gradient Boosting using n1 history and the **same calendar variables**. | Lower MAE than the reduced model and persistence; report failure to improve if that occurs. |\n\n## Data preparation and learning models\n\nStart with the current reading and the previous **55 minutes at five-minute intervals**: twelve readings for each selected signal, at lags 0, 5, …, 55 minutes. Include time of day and day of week as stated in the report. Exact calendar encoding and model settings remain implementation choices. Exclude rows missing any required predictor or whose target lies outside the assigned period. Fit any Ridge scaling on training data only. Correlations and hydraulic reasoning motivate candidates; they do not establish added predictive value.\n\nThe main model, [Gradient Boosting](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.ensemble.GradientBoostingRegressor.html), adds small decision trees that correct earlier prediction errors. Persistence repeats current n1. [Ridge](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.linear_model.Ridge.html) fits a linear relationship while penalizing large coefficients. [Random Forest](https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.ensemble.RandomForestRegressor.html) averages many trees. The comparison asks whether a manageable nonlinear model improves on simple alternatives; these established methods are appropriate to the course's regression and ensemble material.\n\n## Chronological evaluation\n\nTrain on January–September 2018, select settings with October–December 2018, then refit on eligible 2018 rows and assess 2019. Since 2019 has already been inspected in limited ways, call it the **final chronological assessment**, not an untouched holdout. Do not choose settings using 2019 outcomes.\n\nAll models must predict the same eligible rows and the same recorded target. Report **MAE** and **RMSE** in metres. MAE gives average error magnitude; RMSE penalizes large errors more strongly. A boosting advantage requires lower MAE than all three specified comparators. C3 keeps the model family, calendar inputs and evaluation rows fixed so that its full-versus-reduced comparison answers the additional-signal question. Report RMSE tradeoffs and negative findings.\n\nExpected outputs are an annotated preparation/model notebook, a comparison table and forecast/error plots. These remain anticipated outputs. No superiority, useful control improvement or extra-signal benefit is claimed in this repository.\n''')\nwrite('docs/professor_feedback.md','''\n# Response to the Milestone 2 feedback\n\nThe professor found the dataset/task and six-study discussion useful, but requested reproducible evidence for the EDA, topology and 35-group screening; clear attribution; an Ahmed et al. The method includes a **+5-minute pressure predictor** and a current-pressure reconstructor. It trains on leak-free nominal simulations and network connections, using three past five-minute pressure readings and an 80/20 training/validation split. Two networks, each with four Chebyshev graph-convolution layers, use network links to share pressure information. Differences between predicted healthy pressure and current reconstruction are processed over seven days to help locate leaks. The paper compares graph/scaling alternatives (PDF pp. 3–5).\n\n## Implementation actually inspected\n\n[Author repository](https://github.com/GardarGardarsson/GSP_for_Leak_Detection/tree/864a99041af6331375946a8fad3a71c633108eb8), pinned commit `864a99041af6331375946a8fad3a71c633108eb8`.\n\n- [`data_loader.py`, lines 266–293](https://github.com/GardarGardarsson/GSP_for_Leak_Detection/blob/864a99041af6331375946a8fad3a71c633108eb8/04%20Leakage%20Detection/utils/data_loader.py#L266): the input ends at row `i-1`; prediction uses `y[i]`, reconstruction uses `y[i-1]`. These are new L-Town simulations, not the released 2018–2019 SCADA series (preprint PDF pp. 8–10).\n\nSAM-kNN keeps recent examples in short-term memory, older useful examples in long-term memory, and a combined-memory predictor. It chooses the memory with the lowest tracked prediction error and updates online. Predicted-versus-observed pressure deviations support anomaly detection. The method includes a **+5-minute pressure predictor** and a current-pressure reconstructor. It trains on leak-free nominal simulations and network connections, using three past five-minute pressure readings and an 80/20 training/validation split. Two networks, each with four Chebyshev graph-convolution layers, use network links to share pressure information. Differences between predicted healthy pressure and current reconstruction are processed over seven days to help locate leaks. The paper compares graph/scaling alternatives (PDF pp. 3–5).\n\n## Implementation actually inspected\n\n[Author repository](https://github.com/GardarGardarsson/GSP_for_Leak_Detection/tree/864a99041af6331375946a8fad3a71c633108eb8), pinned commit `864a99041af6331375946a8fad3a71c633108eb8`.\n\n- [`data_loader.py`, lines 266–293](https://github.com/GardarGardarsson/GSP_for_Leak_Detection/blob/864a99041af6331375946a8fad3a71c633108eb8/04%20Leakage%20Detection/utils/data_loader.py#L266): the input ends at row `i-1`; prediction uses `y[i]`, reconstruction uses `y[i-1]`. These are new L-Town simulations, not the released 2018–2019 SCADA series (preprint PDF pp. 8–10).\n\nSAM-kNN keeps recent examples in short-term memory, older useful examples in long-term memory, and a combined-memory predictor. It chooses the memory with the lowest tracked prediction error and updates online. Predicted-versus-observed pressure deviations support anomaly detection. Adaptive memories extend ordinary nearest-neighbor regression.\n\n## Implementation actually inspected\n\n[Author code](https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/tree/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54), commit `9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54`.\n\n- [`LeakDbScenarioPreprocessing.py`, lines 19–27](https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/LeakDbScenarioPreprocessing.py#L19): for window length four, average the first three rows and label with the fourth. The latest input is therefore five minutes before the label.\n- [`FaultDetection.py`, lines 40–49](https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/FaultDetection.py#L40): exclude a target sensor from its own predictor inputs.\n- [`experiments.py`](https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/experiments.py#L153): predict the batch before adapting.\n\nThe paper describes four-sample averaging; the inspected code averages three values before the fourth-row label. The licensed [Tsinghua conference report](../papers/P08a_Tsinghua-Team.pdf), [Zenodo source](https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.3924109), is distinct from the journal paper; the journal PDF is not redistributed here.\n\n## Task, data preparation and engineering method\n\nThe task is overlapping-leak detection and isolation, with 2018 development and 2019 evaluation (journal p. 6). STL separates pressure trends from recurring variation; sustained changes and multiscale differences support detection, followed by hydraulic localization. The journal's pressure-trend equation (p. 3, Equation 3) does not specify a fixed future-pressure horizon.\n\nFor Area C, pump inflow minus the tank-storage change rate estimates sector outflow, with summed AMRs as a demand reference. Thirty-minute aggregation reduces level-rounding/inaccuracy effects (p. 7). This is an existing engineering contribution, not a new calculation proposed by the student. The final proposal retains measured T1, pump flow and local AMR_n1; it does not add a tank-balance forecasting experiment.\n\n## Implementation and published result\n\nThe journal's data-availability statement (p. 11) offers code on author request. No executable author code was inspected or run. The earlier conference report mentions VAR but does not establish its numerical lag/order or prospective pressure lead; this cannot be substituted for a verified journal forecast horizon.\n\nJournal p. 11, Conclusions: **true-positive rate 47.83%, five false positives and EUR 167,981**. These are competition leak-diagnosis measures, not numerical future-pressure error. Statements about detected/localized leak candidates should not be equated automatically with accepted competition matches.\n\n## Relation and limits\n\nThe source supports the hydraulic interpretation of Area C's pump/tank boundary and shows prior use of local engineering signals. It does not provide a directly comparable released-SCADA n1(+15) learning result. A controller and a forecast-accuracy improvement are not evaluated in this repository. Training, hydraulic leak localization and competition performance were not reproduced. [Source locators](../../evidence/literature_checks.json) distinguish the checked journal document from its conference predecessor.\n''')\nwrite('literature/ahmed_feedback.md','''\n# Ahmed et al. It does not directly train the proposed future-pressure label. Training uses two selected normal-baseline segments, including a segment extending into January 2019, with a random 15% validation selection of windows; testing starts January 15, 2019. A persistent background leak is treated as normal, so this is not proof of leak-free training. Moving-normalization alignment and overlapping-window dependence matter when comparing protocols (PDF pp. 2–4).\n\nPublished Table 1 (p. 4) reports normal reconstruction errors **0.0732 (CNN)** and **0.0031 (LSTM)**.
